Gaussian universality for approximately polynomial functions of high-dimensional data
Kevin Han Huang, Morgane Austern, Peter Orbanz

Abstract
We establish an invariance principle for polynomial functions of n n independent, high-dimensional random vectors, and also show that the obtained rates are nearly optimal. Both the dimension of the vectors and the degree of the polynomial are permitted to grow with n n . Specifically, we obtain a finite sample upper bound for the error of approximation by a polynomial of Gaussians, measured in Kolmogorov distance, and extend it to functions that are approximately polynomial in a mean squared error sense. We give a corresponding lower bound that shows the invariance principle holds up to polynomial degree o ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) o(\log n) . The proof is constructive and adapts an asymmetrisation argument due to V. V. Senatov. We also give a necessary and sufficient condition for asymptotic normality via the fourth moment phenomenon of Nualart and Peccati. As applications, we obtain a higher-order delta method with possibly non-Gaussian limits, and generalise a number of known results on high-dimensional and infinite-order U-statistics, and on fluctuations of subgraph counts.



,

and

University College London, Harvard University and University College London

Introduction and main results

Gaussian universality results approximate functions of
n
random vectors in distribution by substituting each argument by a
Gaussian surrogate, typically as
n
grows
[
13
,
44
,
38
]
.
Informally, such results are of the form

f_{n}(X_{n1},\ldots,X_{nn})\;\approx\;f_{n}(Z_{n1},\ldots,Z_{nn})\qquad\text{ in distribution for large }n\;,

(1)

where the arguments
X_{ni}
are independent random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d(n)}
for each
{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.
The approximation substitutes independent Gaussian vectors
{Z_{ni}}
, where each
{Z_{ni}}
has the same mean and
covariance as
X_{ni}
. The case where the dimension
d(n)
grows with
n
at a specified rate is commonly referred to
as the high-dimensional regime. Applications of Gaussian universality range from the spectra of large random matrices
[
13
,
58
,
59
,
64
,
3
]
to the risk of neural networks and other high-dimensional regressors
[
38
,
31
,
42
,
28
,
34
]
.

The two main results presented here are (i) a Gaussian universality result for polynomials that upper-bounds the approximation error
in (
1
), and (ii) a lower bound.
Theorem

1
is a finite-sample upper bound that applies if

f_{n}
is a polynomial
{p_{m}:\mathbb{R}^{nd(n)}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}}
of degree
{m(n)\leq n}
.
Both
m(n)
and
d(n)
may grow with
n
.
Since the set of polynomials of degree
\leq m(n)
in the space

{L_{2}=L_{2}(X_{n1},\ldots,X_{nn})}
of square-integrable functions
forms a linear subspace, the bound describes Gaussian universality for functions in this subspace.

Theorem

2
extends it to functions whose
L_{2}
distance to the subspace is small.
We show how the result can be used to generalise results on infinite-order U-statistics

[
60
,
15
,
32
,
63
,
65
,
25
,
5
]

and on fluctuations of subgraph counts
[
33
,
4
,
37
]
.
Since any function in
L_{2}
can be approximated to arbitrary precision by a polynomial of sufficiently high degree,
the result applies in principle to all
L_{2}
functions, but as the degree of the polynomial increases,
the upper bound on the approximation in (
1
) deteriorates. That raises the question whether
the bound can be improved.
Theorem

3
shows that this is, at least in general, not possible:
The bound is essentially optimal over the class of polynomials of degree
\leq m(n)
, for
{m(n)=o(\log n)}
.

Outline

The remainder of this section states the main results (
Section

1.2
) and surveys related work (
Section

1.3
).
Additional universality results on V-statistics follow in
Section

2
,
and applications to the delta method, U-statistics, and random graphs in
Section

3
.
All proofs are in Sections
4
–
10
.
The appendix contains an additional example, and details on some results by Senatov
[
54
]
used in our proofs.

Notation
. Throughout, we suppress dependence on
n
, and use the abbreviations

d\;=\;d(n)\qquad m\;=\;m(n)\qquad X_{i}\;=\;X_{ni}\qquad X\;:=\;(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\,.

The variables
{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}}
are always independent (but not necessarily identically distributed) random elements of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
and
p_{m}
is a polynomial
{\mathbb{R}^{nd}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}}
of degree
m
. By an absolute constant, we mean a constant that does
not depend on
n
,
d
, or
m
, on
p_{m}
, or on the law of
X
.

Main results

To work with polynomials, we must represent powers of random variables. If we substitute a
polynomial
p_{m}
directly into a result of the form (
1
), we obtain an approximation
p_{m}(Z)
,
for a collection of Gaussian vectors

Z\;\coloneqq\;(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})\qquad\text{ where }\quad Z_{1}\condind\ldots\condind Z_{n}\quad\text{ and }\quad Z_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{E}[X_{i}],\text{\rm Var}[X_{i}])\;.

In this case,
{p_{m}(Z)}
computes powers up to degree
m
of each surrogate variable. Our results rely on Lindeberg’s method, which uses a telescoping-sum expansion

\displaystyle f_{n}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})-f_{n}(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})

\displaystyle\;=\;\msum_{i=1}^{n}\big(f_{n}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{i-1},X_{i},Z_{i+1},\ldots,Z_{n})-f_{n}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{i-1},Z_{i},Z_{i+1},\ldots,Z_{n})\big)\;,

followed by a Taylor expansion of each summand with respect to its
i
-th argument. This is directly applicable if
f_{n}
is multilinear, but not if it is a polynomial involving higher powers of
X_{i}
. We reduce the
polynomial to the multilinear case by augmenting the original random vectors: For each
i\leq n
, consider the centred powers of
X_{i}
,

\displaystyle\bar{X}_{i}\;\coloneqq\;X_{i}-\mathbb{E}X_{i}\;,\;\;\;\overline{X_{i}^{\otimes 2}}\coloneqq X_{i}^{\otimes 2}-\mathbb{E}\big[X_{i}^{\otimes 2}\big]\;,\;\;\;\;\;\ldots\;,\;\;\;\;\;\overline{X_{i}^{\otimes m}}\coloneqq X_{i}^{\otimes m}-\mathbb{E}\big[X_{i}^{\otimes m}\big]\;,

and define the concatenated random tensor

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{i}\;\coloneqq\;\begin{pmatrix}\,\bar{X}_{i}\;,\;\overline{X_{i}^{\otimes 2}}\;,\;\ldots\;,\;\overline{X_{i}^{\otimes m}}\,\end{pmatrix}

(2)

with dimension
{D=d+d^{2}+\ldots+d^{m}}
. For a suitable tensor
T_{m}
of polynomial coefficients, we then have

\displaystyle p_{m}(X)-\mathbb{E}[\,p_{m}(X)]\;=\;\langle T_{m}\,,\,(1,\mathbf{X}_{1})^{\top}\otimes\ldots\otimes(1,\mathbf{X}_{n})^{\top}\rangle\;=:\;q_{m}(\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n})\;.

(3)

See
Section

2.1
for details on tensor notation. The function
q_{m}
is multilinear. As surrogates for the “augmented” variables
{\mathbf{X}=(\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n})}
, we choose a collection of Gaussian vectors

\Xi\;\coloneqq\;(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n})\qquad\text{ where }\quad\xi_{1}\condind\ldots\condind\xi_{n}\quad\text{ and }\quad\xi_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}_{i}],\text{\rm Var}[\mathbf{X}_{i}])\;.

Choosing the function
f_{n}
in (
1
) as either
p_{m}
or
q_{m}
then yields the approximations

p_{m}(X)\;\approx\;p_{m}(Z)\qquad\text{ or }\qquad p_{m}(X)\;\approx\;\mathbb{E}[p_{m}(X)]+q_{m}(\Xi)

Although
q_{m}
multiplies
n
coefficients, the tensor
T_{m}
is such that no resulting power exceeds
m
. The variable
q_{m}(\Xi)
is thus a degree-
m
polynomial of Gaussians. The bound is given in terms of the moment terms

\displaystyle\sigma\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\sqrt{\text{\rm Var}\,q_{m}(\mathbf{X})}\;\;

and

\displaystyle\;M_{\nu;i}\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\|\partial_{i}q_{m}(\mathbf{W}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{i}\|_{L_{\nu}}\;,

(4)

where
\mathbf{W}_{i}\coloneqq(\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{i-1},\mathbf{0},\xi_{i+1},\ldots,\xi_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{nD}
and
{\|{\,\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}\,}\|_{L_{\nu}}=(\mathbb{E}|{\,\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}\,}|^{\nu})^{1/\nu}}
.

Theorem 1

(Gaussian universality for polynomials)
.

Fix
\nu\in(2,3]
. Then there exists some absolute constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{P}(\sigma^{-1}q_{m}(\mathbf{X})\leq t)-\mathbb{P}(\sigma^{-1}q_{m}(\Xi)\leq t)\big|\;\leq\;Cm\,\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{ \sum_{i=1}^n M_{\nu;i}^\nu}{ (1+t^2)^{\nu/2} \, \sigma^\nu}$}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\nu m+1}}\;.

for every
n,m,d\in\mathbb{N}
, every
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and every
\sigma>0
.
Moreover, we have
\mathbb{E}[q_{m}(\mathbf{X})]=\mathbb{E}[q_{m}(\Xi)]=0
and
\text{\rm Var}[q_{m}(\mathbf{X})]=\text{\rm Var}[q_{m}(\Xi)]
.

This provides an approximation by
q_{m}(\Xi)
, and we describe where
q_{m}(\Xi)
may be further approximated by
p_{m}(Z)
in
Section

2.3
.

Remark 1
.

A few comments: (i)
Dimension dependence
. The upper bound depends on the dimension
d
,
via the moment terms defined in (
4
).
(ii)
Asymptotic normality
.
Since
q_{m}(\Xi)
is a polynomial of Gaussians, results of Nualart and Peccati
[
49
]

on the fourth moment phenomenon
imply necessary and sufficient conditions
for
q_{m}(\Xi)
to be asymptotically Gaussian. See
Section

2.2
.
(iii)
Monotone transformations
.
Theorem

1
also applies to strictly monotone functions of polynomials (since
it controls a Kolmogorov distance, which is invariant under strictly monotone transforms).
For example,
an exponential of
q_{m}(\mathbf{X})
can be approximated by an exponential of
q_{m}(\Xi)
.

The polynomials of degree
\leq m
in
L_{2}(X)
form a linear subspace of
L_{2}(X)
.
Theorem

1
approximates
functions in this subspace. A simple modification extends the result to functions that are close to the subspace
in the
L_{2}
norm:

Theorem 2

(Approximate polynomials)
.

Fix
\nu\in(2,3]
. There exists some absolute constant
C>0
such that, for every
n,m,d\in\mathbb{N}
and
\sigma>0
,

\displaystyle\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{P}\big(\sigma^{-1}f(X)\leq t\big)-\mathbb{P}\big(\sigma^{-1}

\displaystyle q_{m}(\Xi)\leq t\big)\big|

\displaystyle\;\leq

\displaystyle\;Cm\Big(\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\| f(X) - q_m(\bX) \|_{L_2}}{\sigma}$}}\Big)^{\frac{2}{2m+1}}+\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{ \sum_{i=1}^n M_{\nu;i}^\nu}{ \sigma^\nu}$}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\nu m+1}}\Big)\;

for every measurable function
f:\mathbb{R}^{nd}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

In
Section

3
, we apply this version of the result to obtain:

1.

A high-dimensional delta method. The resulting limit distributions for functions of sample averages
may be non-Gaussian and even non-consistent, due to a phase transition
across approximations by polynomials of Gaussian of different degrees.
See
Proposition

9
for the result and a simple example where such transition happens in high dimensions.

2.

Finite sample bounds that generalise a range of existing results on high-dimensional and infinite-order U-statistics

[
60
,
15
,
32
,
63
,
65
,
25
,
5
]
.
The degree of the U-statistic may grow faster than
\log n
, provided the degree
M
of the approximating polynomial of Gaussians satisfies
M\log M=o(\log n)
. See
Proposition

10
.

3.

Results on fluctuations of subgraph densities at different orders, which extend those in
[
33
,
4
,
37
]
by characterising a full range of vertex-level and edge-level fluctuations with finite-sample bounds. See
Section

3.3
.

Remark 2

(Variance domination)
.

Theorem

2
reduces the three examples above to a single property that we refer to as
variance domination
:
Provided that
\mathbb{E}[f(X)]=0
, the behaviour of
f(X)
matches that of a polynomial
up to an error that depends only on the variance ratio
\text{\rm Var}[f(X)-q_{m}(\mathbf{X})]/\text{\rm Var}[q_{m}(\mathbf{X})]
. In other words,

f(X)
can be approximated by a polynomial if we can extract a polynomial component of
f(X)
that “dominates in variance”.
For the subgraph densities in
Section

3.3
, for example,
variance domination corresponds exactly to known geometric conditions that determine which fluctuation is dominant.

A question not addressed by
Theorem

1
is whether the bound is tight.
Clearly, it may be suboptimal
if additional information about
f_{n}
or the law of
X
is available.
For example, choose the
X_{i}
as
centred i.i.d. random elements of
\mathbb{R}
with unit variance,
p_{m}(X)
as the empirical average

{\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}/n}
, and
\nu=3
. The Berry-Esséen theorem then applies and guarantees
a rate of
{O(n^{-1/2})}
, whereas
Theorem

1
yields

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{\sqrt{n}}$}}\msum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\leq t\Big)-\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{\sqrt{n}}$}}\msum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}\leq t\Big)\Big|\;\leq\;C\,\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{ \| X_1 \|_{L_3} }{ \sqrt{n} \, (1+t^2)^{3/2}
}$}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}\;=\;O(n^{-1/8})\;.

If the only assumption made in (
1
) is that
f_{n}
is polynomial of degree
{\leq m}
, however, the bound is essentially tight:

Theorem 3

(Lower bound)
.

Fix
\nu\in(2,3]
, and assume that
m
is even with
m=o(\log n)
.
Then there exist a sequence of probability measures
\mu^{(n)}_{\nu,\sigma_{0}}
, a sequence of polynomials
{p^{*}_{m}=p^{*}_{m(n)}}
, and
absolute constants
{c,C>0}
and
{N\in\mathbb{N}}
, such that

\displaystyle cn^{-\frac{\nu-2}{2\nu m}}\;\leq

\displaystyle\;\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{P}(p^{*}_{m}(X)\leq t)-\mathbb{P}(p^{*}_{m}(Z)\leq t)\big|\;\leq\;Cmn^{-\frac{\nu-2}{2\nu m+2}}\;,

\displaystyle cn^{-\frac{\nu-2}{2\nu m}}\;\leq

\displaystyle\;\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{P}\big(q^{*}_{m}(\mathbf{X})\leq t\big)-\mathbb{P}\big(q^{*}_{m}(\Xi)\leq t\big)\big|\;\leq\;Cmn^{-\frac{\nu-2}{2\nu m+2}}

for i.i.d. variables
{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\sim\mu^{(n)}_{\nu,\sigma_{0}}}
and all
n\geq N
.

See
Section

6
. The proof is inspired by an asymmetric construction by Senatov
[
54
]
that controls the probability of a centred, multivariate average lying in a sequence of uncentred Euclidean balls. For our problem, a different asymmetrisation is required to obtain a comparable lower bound to the upper bound from
Theorem

1
.

Section

5

motivates the proof and constructs
(\mu^{(n)}_{\nu,\sigma_{0}})
and
(p_{m}^{*})
explicitly.
∎

Remark 3
.

For a generic function
f_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{nd}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
Theorem

2
establishes a universality result when
f_{n}
is well-approximated by its
m
-th order Taylor expansion.
Theorem

3
shows that this Taylor approximation, which becomes more accurate
as
m
grows, trades off against the distributional approximation in (
1
),
which deteriorates with
m
. In other words, when no additional information about
f_{n}
or the law of
X
is available, the error bound obtained from
Theorem

1
is near-optimal, and becomes tighter as
m
grows.

Related work

A substantial body of work exists on Gaussian universality and its applications. This section briefly surveys results directly relevant to ours.

Lindeberg’s principle.
Gaussian universality results are typically proved using Lindeberg’s method. This method was generalised beyond the central limit theorem by Rotar
[
52
,
51
]
, by Mossel, O’Donnell, and Oleszkiewicz
[
43
,
44
]
, and by Chatterjee

[
13
]
.
Compared to distribution-specific techniques, such as Fourier analysis or Stein’s method, it has the advantage that it adapts easily to nonlinear estimators with unknown asymptotic distributions. On the other hand, it is known to result in sub-optimal error rates for a range of specific problems (see the discussion after Theorem 3.3 in
[
14
]
for empirical averages, and the remark in Section 4 of
[
9
]
for random matrices). This sub-optimality is due to too much smoothing: Lindeberg’s principle approximates the Kolmogorov metric by an integral probability metric, using test functions with a second derivative that is
{(\nu-2)}
-Hölder for some
{\nu>2}
.
Theorem

3
shows this sub-optimality is a necessary price of generality: For the general class of
n
-dependent probability measures, the bounds obtained by Lindeberg’s principle are, in fact, near-optimal.

Invariance principle for general multilinear forms.

The use of Lindeberg’s method for multilinear forms goes back to Rotar
[
52
,
51
]
, who
obtained asymptotic approximations by polynomials of Gaussian for fixed
m
and
d
. By additionally employing an inequality of Carbery and Wright
[
11
]
, Mossel et al.
[
44
]
obtain a finite-sample Kolmogorov-distance bound for univariate data distributions. Recent work also considers convergence in total variation, in both the asymptotic
[
48
]
and the finite-sample regime
[
2
,
39
]
.
Our work focuses on the regime with large
m
and
d
, and our lower bound in Kolmogorov distance directly implies a lower bound in total variation.
Another line of work adapts the multivariate Stein’s method to general functions of asymptotically normal random vectors, where the error bounds explicitly sum over the
d
different coordinates of the random vectors
[
26
,
27
]
. These results either explicitly assume
m
and
d
to be fixed, or impose implicit requirements that may be too strong for applications. Indeed, the main technical work behind
Theorem

1
and its applications is to obtain tight dependence on
d(n)
and
m(n)
.

Error bounds for Gaussian universality.
The lower bound in
Theorem

3
helps to explain similar rates that have been observed in the upper bound for a number of problems. For the problem of non-Gaussian approximation of a degree-two U-statistic with kernel
u
, the known rates depend on the number of non-zero eigenvalues of the Hilbert-Schmidt operator associated with
u
:
O(n^{-1})
for five non-zero eigenvalues
[
29
]
,
O(n^{-1/12})
for one non-zero eigenvalue
[
66
]
, and
O(n^{-1/14})
without eigenvalue assumptions
[
35
]
. It is also conjectured in
[
66
]
that
O(n^{-1/12})
is optimal, based on a result by Senatov
[
54
]
that the Gaussian approximation of a heavy-tailed multivariate average in a carefully chosen sequence of Euclidean balls is
\Omega(n^{-1/12})
. The proof of
Theorem

3
relies on adapting an asymmetry argument by
[
54
]
(see
Section

5
for a sketch of intuition).
Theorem

3
(with
\nu=3
) does not directly address the conjecture by
[
66
]
: We provide a rate between
n^{-1/6m}
and
mn^{-1/(6m+2)}
, but for a mixture of a heavy-tailed average and a degree-
m
V-statistic of Gaussians. We do not know whether a similar lower bound holds for degree-
m
U-statistics.

Distribution approximations for high-dimensional statistics.

The approximation of specific estimators in high-dimensional regimes is the subject of an extensive literature. Such regimes differ by
the parameters that grow with
n
. Examples include degree-two U-statistics with
d=d(n)

[
15
,
63
,
65
,
25
,
35
]
, quadratic forms with
n
-dependent weights

[
5
]
, infinite-order U-statistics with
m=m(n)

[
60
,
56
]
, and statistics with high-dimensional outputs
[
17
,
16
,
56
]
. Our results assume univariate outputs, but allow general
dependence of
d
,
m
and weights of the polynomial on
n
. The extension to functions that can be approximated by polynomials in an
L_{2}
sense, in
Theorem

2
, plays a key role in our applications. For additional references on U-statistics and subgraph count statistics, and more detailed comparisons to our results, see
Section

3
.

Terminology: Gaussian universality.
We use the term Gaussian universality to refer to the approximation of a function of
(X_{i})_{i\leq n}
by the same function of Gaussian vectors
(Z_{i})_{i\leq n}
; this
matches the nomenclature of most of the literature surveyed above.
Some texts—for example, in the context of Gaussian approximation of Wiener chaos—use the term instead to indicate that
the overall function is asymptotically normal, see e.g. Chapter 11 of
[
46
]
. Since our approximation
q_{m}(\Xi)
still involves
n
-dependent quantities, it may have Gaussian or non-Gaussian limits (
Proposition

4
). We also note that our results take the form of a penultimate approximation. Other examples of penultimate approximations include chi-squared and shifted-gamma distributions as approximations for the Gaussian CLT limit
[
30
,
8
]
and the Type-III extreme value distribution for extreme value theorem
[
18
]
. While those penultimate approximations typically yield sharper approximation rates than the final limits (Gaussian or extreme value distributions), our approximations do not.

Additional results on universality

This section first makes the definition of the variables
\mathbf{X}_{i}
precise in
Section

2.1
.

Section

2.2
applies the fourth moment theorem
[
47
]
to give conditions for Gaussianity of the
limiting distribution.
Section

2.3
shows that, under a mild condition, the approximations by
{p_{m}(Z)}
and by
{\mathbb{E}[p_{m}(X)]+q_{m}(\mathbf{X})}

are asymptotically equivalent for simple V-statistics that arise for example from the delta method.

Approximation by polynomials of Gaussian: details

To specify polynomials of vectors, we use tensor notation:
By a tensor
T
of size
{d_{1}\times\ldots\times d_{k}}
, we mean an element
of
{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\ldots\times d_{k}}}
.
The tensor product of
k
vectors
{x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}}
is the tensor
\otimes_{i}x_{i}
with entries

\otimes_{i\leq k}\,x_{i}({j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}})\;=\;x_{1}({j_{1}})\cdots x_{k}({j_{k}})\quad\text{ for }j_{1}\leq d_{1},\ldots,j_{k}\leq d_{k}\;,

and we write
{x_{1}^{\otimes r}:=\otimes_{i\leq r}\,x_{1}}
for short. The scalar product of two tensors of equal size is

\langle S,T\rangle\;:=\;\msum_{i_{1}\leq d_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\leq d_{k}}S(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})T(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})\qquad\text{ for }S,T\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\ldots\times d_{k}}\;,

or equivalently the Euclidean scalar product in
{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\ldots\times d_{k}}}
.
Any polynomial
p_{m}
of degree
{\leq m}
can be represented as

\displaystyle p_{m}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\;=\;T_{0}+\msum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1}+\ldots+r_{n}\leq m\end{subarray}}\langle T_{r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}}\,,\,x_{1}^{\otimes r_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes x_{n}^{\otimes r_{n}}\rangle\;,

where
T_{0}
is a scalar and each
T_{r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}}
is a tensor of size
{d^{r_{1}}\times\ldots\times d^{r_{n}}}
.
Having defined the augmented random tensor
\mathbf{X}_{i}
in (
2
), we can write
p_{m}
as

\displaystyle p_{m}(X)\;=\;\mathbb{E}[\,p_{m}(X)]\,+\,\langle T_{m}\,,\,(1,\mathbf{X}_{1})^{\top}\otimes\ldots\otimes(1,\mathbf{X}_{n})^{\top}\rangle

(5)

for a tensor
T_{m}
.
T_{m}
is determined completely by
T_{0}
and
(T_{r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}})_{r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}\leq m}
. Since
p_{m}
has degree
m
,
T_{m}
is such that for
m^{\prime}>m
, all
m^{\prime}
-fold products of
\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
correspond to zero coefficients in
T_{m}
.

Throughout the article, we also make use of the tensor vectorisation operator. For a tensor
T
of size
d_{1}\times\ldots\times d_{k}
, we define the corresponding flattened vector as

\displaystyle\text{\rm vec}(T)\;\coloneqq\;\big(\,T(1,\ldots,1,1)\,,\,T(1,\ldots,1,2)\,,\,\ldots\,,\,T(d_{1},\ldots,d_{k-1},d_{k})\,\big)^{\top}\;\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\cdots d_{k}}\;.

Gaussianity and the fourth moment phenomenon

To understand whether
p_{m}(X)
may be asymptotically Gaussian,
Theorem

1
reduces the problem to studying the asymptotic normality of
q_{m}(\Xi)
, a degree-
m
polynomial of
nd
i.i.d. univariate standard normal variables. This polynomial lives in the span of products of Hermite polynomials with total degree
\leq m
, and is therefore an
m
-th order Wiener chaos; see
[
45
]
for an introduction. If
m
is fixed, the fourth moment theorem by Nualart and Peccati
[
49
]
applies and shows that
q_{m}(\Xi)
is asymptotically Gaussian as
n\rightarrow\infty
if and only if its excess kurtosis, defined as

\displaystyle\text{\rm Kurt}[q_{m}(\Xi)]\;\coloneqq\;\mathbb{E}[(q_{m}(\Xi)-\mathbb{E}[q_{m}(\Xi)])^{4}]\,/\,\text{\rm Var}[q_{m}(\Xi)]^{2}-3\;=\;\sigma^{-4}\,\mathbb{E}[(q_{m}(\Xi))^{4}]-3\;,

is asymptotically zero. Denote the total variation distance by
d_{\rm TV}
. By directly using a finite-sample bound developed by Nourdin and Peccati
[
47
]
, the next result says that this is sufficient even as
m
grows, and is necessary under an uniform integrability condition.

Proposition 4

(Fourth moment phenomenon)
.

Let
\eta
be a univariate standard normal variable independent of all other quantities. For every
n,m,d\in\mathbb{N}
and
\sigma>0
, we have

\displaystyle d_{\rm TV}(\sigma^{-1}q_{m}(\Xi)\,,\,\eta)\;\leq

\displaystyle\;\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{4m-4}{3m}$}}\,\big|\text{\rm Kurt}[q_{m}(\Xi)]\big|\Big)^{1/2}\;.

Under the high-dimensional asymptotic regime, as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

(i)

\text{\rm Kurt}[q_{m}(\Xi)]\rightarrow 0
is a sufficient condition for
\sigma^{-1}q_{m}(\Xi)\overset{d}{\rightarrow}\eta
;

(ii)

if
\sigma^{-4}q_{m}(\Xi)^{4}
is uniformly integrable, then
\text{\rm Kurt}[q_{m}(\Xi)]\rightarrow 0
is also necessary.

The finite-sample bound is a restatement of Theorem 1.1 of Nourdin and Peccati
[
47
]
for
q_{m}(\Xi)
, and directly implies (i). (ii) is proved by noting that, when
\sigma^{-1}q_{m}(\Xi)\overset{d}{\rightarrow}\eta
, by continuous mapping theorem,
\sigma^{-4}q_{m}(\Xi)^{4}\overset{d}{\rightarrow}\eta^{4}
. Uniform integrability then implies the desired moment convergence (see Theorem 25.12 of
[
6
]
).
∎

Remark 4
.

Since the bound from
[
47
]
works for a general sequence of Wiener chaos,
Proposition

4
also holds with
\sigma^{-1}q_{m}(\Xi)
replaced by
\text{\rm Var}[p_{m}(Z)]^{-1/2}(p_{m}(Z)-\mathbb{E}[p_{m}(Z)])
.

A consequence of
Theorem

1
and
Proposition

4
is that, even if
p_{m}(X)
is not asymptotically Gaussian when
d
and
m
are fixed, it can become Gaussian in the high-dimensional regime. Similar phenomenon has already been observed for U-statistics
[
36
,
5
]
. We defer to
Remark

12
in
Section

3.1
to illustrate this intuition through a toy example.

Simple V-statistics

In
Section

1.2
, we have motivated the approximation of
p_{m}(X)-\mathbb{E}[p_{m}(X)]
by
q_{m}(\Xi)
, a polynomial of the augmented variables, by noting how
q_{m}
adapts well to Lindeberg’s principle. On the other hand, in the case of
p_{m}(X)=g(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i})
, the continuous mapping theorem suggests an approximation by
p_{m}(Z)
, a polynomial of the original variables, at least when
g
and
d
are fixed in
n
. A natural question is whether the two approximations, i.e. the two different notions of Gaussian universality, coincide. Note that in the high-dimensional regime, it is non-trivial to show that one may replace
X_{i}
’s by
Z_{i}
’s in
p_{m}
:
[
26
,
27
]
have used Stein’s method and a Lipschitz argument to control the approximation error by
p_{m}(Z)
in smooth function bounds, but those bounds are only well-controlled when dimension
d
grows sublinearly in
n
and not well-adapted to the high-dimensional setting.

In this section, we confirm that the two notions of universality do coincide in the high-dimensional regime, under a mild moment condition and for simple V-statistics. We focus on the case where
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
are i.i.d. zero-mean and consider, for
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the statistic

\displaystyle v_{m}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\;\coloneqq\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n^m}$}}\msum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}\in[n]}\langle S,\,x_{i_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes x_{i_{m}}\rangle\;,

(6)

where
S\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{m}}
is a deterministic, symmetric tensor. Note that this is exactly the dominating quantity under an
m
-th order delta method, and when
S
is not required to be symmetric, any
p_{m}(X)
of the form (
5
) can be written as a weighted sum of such V-statistics.

The moment condition is cumbersome to state, but can be motivated as follows. We first notice that for the distributions of
p_{m}(Z)
and
q_{m}(\Xi)
to agree, we require the contribution of terms like
Z_{i}^{\otimes m}
(found in
p_{m}(Z)
) and
\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{E}[X_{i}^{\otimes m}],\text{\rm Var}[X_{i}^{\otimes m}])
(found in
q_{m}(\Xi)
) to be negligible. To make the condition well-adapted to a setting where
d
and the law of
X_{i}
’s may vary in
n
, we need careful controls for all possible
m
-fold products of
Z_{i}
’s and of
X_{i}
’s with at least one repeated element. We coin this moment condition
\delta
-regularity:

Definition 5
.

v_{m}
is
\delta
-regular with respect to
X
if, for some
\delta\in[0,1)
, there exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that for all
j\in[m-1]
,

\displaystyle\max_{q_{1}+\ldots+q_{j}=m\,,\,q_{l}\in\mathbb{N}}\max\bigg\{\text{\rm Var}\bigg[\bigg\langle S\,,\,\bigotimes_{l=1}^{j}X_{l}^{\otimes q_{l}}\bigg\rangle\bigg]\,,\,\text{\rm Var}\bigg[\bigg\langle S\,,\,

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{l=1}^{j}Z_{l}^{\otimes q_{l}}\bigg\rangle\bigg]\bigg\}

(7)

\displaystyle\;\leq

\displaystyle\;Cn^{m+\delta}m^{-j}\,\text{\rm Var}[v_{m}(X)]\;,

\displaystyle\max_{q_{1}+\ldots+q_{j}=m\,,\,q_{l}\in\mathbb{N}}\max\bigg\{\bigg(\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg\langle S\,,\,\bigotimes_{l=1}^{j}X_{l}^{\otimes q_{l}}\bigg\rangle\bigg]\bigg)^{2}\,,\,\bigg(\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg\langle S\,,\,

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{l=1}^{j}Z_{l}^{\otimes q_{l}}\bigg\rangle\bigg]\bigg)^{2}\bigg\}

(8)

\displaystyle\;\leq

\displaystyle\;C\,n^{m+\delta}\,\text{\rm Var}[v_{m}(X)]\;.

Remark 5
.

The
n^{m}
factor on the RHS balances the variance of the un-normalised V-statistic
v_{m}(X)
. The
m^{-j}
factor accounts for both the
\binom{m-1}{j-1}=O(m^{j-1})
multiplicity of the terms in
\text{\rm Var}[v_{m}(X)]
that take the form of the LHS, and the fact that the moment of a univariate standard Gaussian
\eta
grows as
\mathbb{E}[\eta^{m}]=O(m^{m/2})
. In
Lemma

25
, we provide a verification of
\delta
-regularity for a degree-
m
V-statistic of univariate Gaussians as part of the proof of
Theorem

3
. Sufficient conditions for verifying
\delta
-regularity are deferred to
Remark

8
. There, we show that when
m
and
d
are fixed, up to a spectral condition,
\delta
-regularity always holds. It suggests that the requirement of
\delta
-regularity is specific to our high-dimensional setup, where both
m
and
d
may grow in
n
.

We are ready to state the main result of this subsection, which confirms that under
\delta
-regularity for the V-statistic
v_{m}
in (
6
), the approximations by
q_{m}(\Xi)
and
p_{m}(Z)-\mathbb{E}[p_{m}(X)]
do agree. Here and below, we let
q^{v}_{m}
be the multilinear representation of
v_{m}
defined in (
3
).

Proposition 6
.

Fix
\nu\in(2,3]
and let
(X_{i})_{i\leq n}
be i.i.d. zero-mean. If
v_{m}
is
\delta
-regular with respect to
X
for some
\delta\in[0,1)
, there exist some absolute constants
C,C^{\prime}>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{P}\big(\sigma^{-1}(q^{v}_{m}(\Xi)+\mathbb{E}[v_{m}(X)])\leq t\big)-\mathbb{P}\big(\sigma^{-1}v_{m}(Z)\leq t\big)\big|\,\;\leq\;\,Cmn^{-\frac{1-\delta}{2m+1}}\;,

\displaystyle\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{P}(\sigma^{-1}v_{m}(X)\leq t)-\mathbb{P}(\sigma^{-1}v_{m}(Z)\leq t)\big|\,\;\leq\;\,Cmn^{-\frac{1-\delta}{2m+1}}+C^{\prime}m\Delta_{\delta}\;,

\displaystyle\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{P}(\sigma^{-1}(v_{m}(X)-\mathbb{E}[v_{m}(X)])\leq t)-\mathbb{P}(\sigma^{-1}(v_{m}(Z)-\mathbb{E}[v_{m}(Z)])\leq t)\big|

\displaystyle\hskip 200.0003pt\,\;\leq\;\,Cmn^{-\frac{1-\delta}{2m+1}}+C^{\prime}m\Delta_{\delta}\;,

where the error term is defined as

\displaystyle\Delta_{\delta}\;\coloneqq\;\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{ \sum_{i=1}^n M_{\nu;i}^\nu}{ \sigma^\nu}$}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\nu m+1}}\;.

Remark 6
.

(i)
Proposition

6
is proved by an adaptation of variance domination in
Theorem

2
together with the variance ratio bounds in
Lemma

7
. (ii) While
\mathbb{E}[v_{m}(X)]
does not necessarily equal
\mathbb{E}[v_{m}(Z)]
, the third bound of
Proposition

6
implies that the difference is asymptotically negligible. (iii) An explicit bound on
\Delta_{\delta}
is given in
Lemma

8
.

The proof of
Proposition

6
is included in
Section

9
, and an illustration of Gaussian universality for a toy V-statistic in
Section

A.1
. The proof makes use of the next two lemmas, which are also useful for subsequent applications. Their proofs are included in
Section

9
. The first lemma shows that
\delta
-regularity can be used to control two variance ratios, which are similar to the moment ratio obtained from variance domination (
Theorem

2
):

Lemma 7
.

Suppose
(X_{i})_{i\leq n}
are i.i.d. zero-mean. Assume that
v_{m}
is
\delta
-regular with respect to
X
for some
\delta\in[0,1)
. Also assume a coupling between
\xi_{i}
and
Z_{i}
such that
\xi_{i1}=Z_{i}
almost surely. Then there exists some absolute constants
C,C_{*}>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{ \| q^v_m(\Xi) + \mean[v_m(X)] - v_m(Z) \|_{L_2}^2 }{\Var[q^v_m(\Xi)]}$}}\;\leq\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{ C^m }{ n^{1-\delta}}$}}

and
\big(1-(C_{*})^{m}n^{-(1-\delta)/2}\big)^{2}\leq\frac{\text{\rm Var}[v_{m}(Z)]}{\text{\rm Var}[v_{m}(X)]}\;\leq\;\big(1+(C_{*})^{m}n^{-(1-\delta)/2}\big)^{2}
.

The next lemma applies
Theorem

1
to show Gaussian universality with respect to the augmented variables
\mathbf{X}
, and use the simple structure of
v_{m}
to bound the moment ratio explicitly. The error bounds are given in terms of both
M_{\nu;i}
defined in
Theorem

1
for
q^{v}_{m}
and

\displaystyle\alpha_{\nu}(S,k)\;\coloneqq\;\bigg\|\msum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}+\ldots+p_{k}=m\\
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\Big\langle S\,,\,\overline{X_{1}^{\otimes p_{1}}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\overline{X_{k}^{\otimes p_{k}}}\,\Big\rangle\bigg\|_{L_{\nu}}\quad\text{ for }\nu\in[2,3]\text{ and }k\in[m]\;.

We also provide upper and lower bounds on
\text{\rm Var}[v_{m}(X)]
in terms of
\alpha_{2}(S,k)
.

Lemma 8
.

Fix
\nu\in(2,3]
and let
(X_{i})_{i\leq n}
be i.i.d. zero-mean. Then there exists some absolute constant
C>0
such that, for every
n,m,d\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
\sigma=\text{\rm Var}[v_{m}(X)]>0
,

\displaystyle\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{P}(\sigma^{-1}q^{v}_{m}(\mathbf{X})\leq t)-\mathbb{P}(\sigma^{-1}q^{v}_{m}(\Xi)\leq t)\big|\;\leq\;Cm\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{ \sum_{i=1}^n M_{\nu;i}^\nu}{ \sigma^\nu}$}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\nu m+1}}\;=\;Cm\Delta_{\delta}\;.

Meanwhile, if
n\geq 2m^{2}
, there exist absolute constants
C^{\prime},C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\Delta_{\delta}\;\leq\;C^{\prime}\,n^{-\frac{\nu-2}{2\nu m+2}}\,\beta_{m,\nu}^{\frac{\nu}{2\nu m+2}}\;,\qquad\beta_{m,\nu}\;\coloneqq\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{
\sum_{k=1}^m
\binom{n}{k}
(\alpha_\nu(S, k))^2
}{
\sum_{k=1}^m
\binom{n}{k}
(\alpha_2(S,k))^2
}$}}\;,

\displaystyle\text{ and }\quad\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{(C_1)^m}{n^{2m}}$}}\msum_{k=1}^{m}\Bigl(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}$}}\Bigr)\,(\alpha_{2}(S,k))^{2}\;\leq\;\text{\rm Var}[v_{m}(X)]\;\leq\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{(C_2)^m}{n^{2m}}$}}\msum_{k=1}^{m}\Bigl(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}$}}\Bigr)\,(\alpha_{2}(S,k))^{2}\;.

Remark 7
.

The moment bound in
Lemma

8
involves a ratio of the
\nu
-th moment versus the second moment of the same quantity. In fact, the ratio is a strict generalisation of the moment ratio from the classical Berry-Esséen bound for sample averages (
m=1
), and similar to the Berry-Esséen ratio, we expect it to be
O(1)
for distributions that are not too heavy-tailed. A crude upper bound on this ratio is deferred to
Remark

9
.

Remark 8

(Sufficient condition for verifying
\delta
-regularity)
.

A sufficient condition for
\delta
-regularity can be obtained from a moment condition and a spectral condition. Recall that
\text{\rm vec}(T)
is the vectorisation of a tensor
T
(
Section

2.1
), and let
\lambda_{\rm max}(A)
and
\lambda_{\rm min}(A)
denote the maximum and minimum eigenvalues of a real symmetric matrix
A
. Suppose there exist
\omega^{(1)}_{\rm max},\omega^{(2)}_{\rm max}>0
such that for all
j\leq m-1
and any
q_{1},\ldots,q_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
that sum up to
m
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\rm max}\Big(\text{\rm Var}\Big[\text{\rm vec}\Big(\otimes_{l=1}^{j}X_{l}^{\otimes q_{l}}\Big)\Big]\Big)\;\leq

\displaystyle\;\omega^{(1)}_{\rm max}\;,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\rm max}\Big(\mathbb{E}\Big[\text{\rm vec}\Big(\otimes_{l=1}^{j}X_{l}^{\otimes q_{l}}\Big)\text{\rm vec}\Big(\otimes_{l=1}^{j}X_{l}^{\otimes q_{l}}\Big)^{\top}\Big]\Big)\;\leq

\displaystyle\;\omega^{(2)}_{\rm max}\;.

and suppose
\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{2})>0
, where

\displaystyle\Sigma_{2}\;\coloneqq\;\text{\rm Var}[\text{\rm vec}(Q_{X})]\;,\qquad\qquad Q_{X}\;\coloneqq\;\msum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}+\ldots+p_{k}=m\\
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\overline{X_{1}^{\otimes p_{1}}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\overline{X_{k}^{\otimes p_{k}}}\;\;.

WLOG take
\|\text{\rm vec}(S)\|=1
. Then we have

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:delta:reg:var}\;\leq\;\omega^{(1)}_{\rm max}\;,\qquad\qquad\eqref{eq:delta:reg:mean}\;\leq\;\omega^{(2)}_{\rm max}\;,\qquad\qquad\text{\rm Var}[v_{m}(X)]\;\geq\;C^{m}n^{-m}\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{2})

for some absolute constant
C>0
provided that
n\geq 2m^{2}
; the third bound is obtained from
Lemma

8
. Since
m=o(\log(n))
,
C^{m}=O(n^{\epsilon})
for any
\epsilon>0
, so to show
\delta
-regularity, it suffices to check that for some constant
C>0
, some
\delta\in[0,1)
and some
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\max\{\omega^{(1)}_{\rm max}\,,\,\omega^{(2)}_{\rm max}\}\;\leq

\displaystyle\;C\,n^{\delta-\epsilon}\,\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{2})\,.

Notice that
\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{2})
is the minimum eigenvalue of the covariance matrix of
X_{1}
when
m=1
. Moreover, when
m
and
d
are fixed, up to a positive minimum eigenvalue condition,
\delta
-regularity always holds. We emphasise that the above only gives crude bounds on the quantities in
Definition

5
. Instead of taking the minimum eigenvalue of
\Sigma_{2}
, it can often be easier to compute
\text{\rm Var}[v_{m}(X)]
directly: One approach is to decompose a V-statistic as a sum of U-statistics (see
Lemma

30
) followed by using known moment formulas for U-statistics (see
Section

8
). Such an approach is used in
Lemma

25
as part of the proof of
Theorem

3
.

Remark 9

(Upper bound on the moment ratio in
Lemma

8
)
.

We again use a spectral condition. Consider
\Sigma_{2}
and
Q_{X}
defined in
Remark

8
, and the analogue of
\Sigma_{2}
but with a fourth moment:

\displaystyle\Sigma_{4}\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{E}\Big[\,\text{\rm vec}\big(Q_{X}^{\otimes 2}\big)\,\text{\rm vec}\big(Q_{X}^{\otimes 2}\big)^{\top}\,\Big]\;.

Define
\lambda_{\rm max}
and
\lambda_{\rm min}
as in
Remark

8
and suppose
\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{2})>0
. Then by noting
\nu<4
, an upper bound on the moment ratio in
Lemma

8

is given as

\displaystyle\beta_{m,\nu}\;\leq\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{ \lambda_{\rm max}(\Sigma_4)^{1/2} }{ \lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_2) }$}}\;.

Applications

In this section, we obtain generalisations of several existing results as applications of our theorems. For notational simplicity, we mostly consider i.i.d. data and symmetric functions. As our general results also hold in the non-i.i.d. and asymmetric case, the interesting phenomena we observe can be readily extended, and
Section

3.3
provides one such example. Notably, since any degree-
m
, symmetric polynomial of
n
variables can be written as a weighted sum of U-statistics and V-statistics with degree
\leq m
, all our applications will be reduced to studying the asymptotic distributions of U-statistics and V-statistics. The proofs for all results in this section are included in
Section

10
.

Higher-order delta method

We first extend the classical delta method
[
50
,
7
]
to a high-dimensional setup. Assume that
(X_{i})_{i\leq n}
are i.i.d. and, for some smooth function
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, define

\displaystyle\hat{g}(X)\;\coloneqq\;g\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n}$}}\msum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\Big)\;=\;g\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n}$}}\msum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{X}_{i}+\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]\Big)\;,\qquad\text{ where }\bar{X}_{i}=X_{i}-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]\;.

\hat{g}(X)
is called a plug-in estimator of
g(\mathbb{E}X_{1})
. A guiding example is
g_{\rm toy}(x)\coloneqq x^{\top}x
, which yields a simple V-statistic

\displaystyle\hat{g}_{\rm toy}(X)\;\coloneqq\;g_{\rm toy}\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n}$}}\msum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\Big)\;=\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n^2}$}}\msum_{i,j=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\top}X_{j}\;.

To see how the distribution of the plug-in estimator behaves through
variance domination, consider a Taylor expansion

\displaystyle\hat{g}_{\rm toy}(X)-g_{\rm toy}(\mathbb{E}[X_{1}])\;=\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{2}{n}$}}\msum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{X}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]\,+\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n^2}$}}\msum_{i,j=1}^{n}\bar{X}_{i}^{\top}\bar{X}_{j}\;.

The variances of the first and second-order expansions are respectively on the order

\displaystyle\Theta\Big(

\displaystyle\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\mean[X_1]^\top\Var[X_1] \mean[X_1]}{n}$}}\Big)\;

and

\displaystyle\;\Theta\Big(

\displaystyle\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\Var[ \bar X_1^\top\bar X_2]}{n^2}$}}+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\Var[\bar X_1^\top\bar X_1]}{n^3}$}}\Big)\;.

(9)

Classically when
d
is fixed, normality of the plug-in estimator follows from the first-order delta method whenever
\partial g(\mathbb{E}[X_{1}])\neq 0
. For
\hat{g}_{\rm toy}(X)
, this condition is equivalent to
\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]\neq 0
, and exactly corresponds to when the first-order Taylor expansion dominates in variance. In the high-dimensional regime, however, the moment terms above can vary with
n
through their
d
-dependence: Take
X_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,I_{d})
with
\|\mu\|_{2}=1
, in which case (
9
) becomes

\displaystyle\Theta\Big(

\displaystyle\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n}$}}\Big)

and

\displaystyle\Theta\Big(

\displaystyle\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{d}{n^2}$}}+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{d}{n^3}$}}\Big)\;.

Even though
\mu=\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]
and therefore
\partial g(\mathbb{E}[X_{1}])
is non-zero, the first-order term is still dominated in variance by the second-order term when
d=\omega(\sqrt{n})
: The first-order delta method fails to hold, and the plug-in estimator is no longer asymptotically normal. We now formalise this in a result concerning an
m
-th order delta method.

Derivatives.
A delta method relies on the derivatives of
g
, and some shorthands are introduced next.
For

x,x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

and

m\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, we define

\displaystyle g_{m}^{(x)}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{m!}$}}\big\langle\partial_{m}\,g(x)\,,\,(x_{1}-\mathbb{E}X_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes(x_{m}-\mathbb{E}X_{1})\big\rangle

\displaystyle\hat{g}_{m}^{(\mathbb{E}X_{1})}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;n^{-m}\,\msum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}\in[n]}\,g_{m}^{(\mathbb{E}X_{1})}\big(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{m}}\big)\;.

which is a degree-
m
polynomial function. If
g
is
(m+1)
-times continuously differentiable everywhere, we can perform an
m
-th order Taylor expansion with the integral remainder. Let
S_{n}\coloneqq n^{-1}\sum_{i\leq n}\bar{X}_{i}
and draw
\Theta\sim\textrm{Uniform}[0,1]
independently of
X
. Then almost surely

\displaystyle\hat{g}(X)\;=\;g(\mathbb{E}X_{1})+\msum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{g}_{j}^{(\mathbb{E}X_{1})}(X)\,+\,(m+1)\mathbb{E}\big[(1-\Theta)^{m+1}\,\hat{g}_{m+1}^{(\mathbb{E}X_{1}+\Theta S_{n})}(X)\,\big|\,X\big]\;.

Note that when
\hat{g}_{m+1}^{(\mathbb{E}X_{1}+\Theta S_{n})}
is independent of
\Theta
, the
(m+1)
factor is cancelled out by
\mathbb{E}[(1-\Theta)^{m+1}]=(m+1)^{-1}
. The penultimate approximation will be identified by comparing which term of the Taylor expansion dominates in variance.

For simplicity of notation, we will assume that
\hat{g}^{(\mathbb{E}X_{1})}_{m}
is
\delta
-regular with respect to
(\bar{X}_{i})_{i\leq n}
(see
Definition

5
and
Remark

8
for a sufficient condition), so that we can apply universality directly to
X
and not just the augmented variables. In the case of
g_{\rm toy}
with
\bar{X}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
, this corresponds to requiring the variance term involving
\bar{X}_{1}^{\top}\bar{X}_{1}
to be negligible, which always holds in view of (
9
).

Moments.
The result involves several moment terms. For each
m\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, define

\displaystyle\mu_{m}\;\coloneqq\;\mathbb{E}\,\hat{g}_{m}^{(\mathbb{E}X_{1})}(X)\;.

Note that
\mu_{0}=g(\mathbb{E}X_{1})
, which is the target of estimation under the plug-in principle. We also define, for
\nu\in(2,3]
and
k\in[m]
, the moment terms

\displaystyle\epsilon_{m}\;\coloneqq\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\Var\Big[ \sum_{l=1}^{m-1} \hat g^{(\mean X_1)}_{l}(X) \Big]}{\Var\Big[ \hat g_m^{(\mean X_1)}(X) \Big]}$}}+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{
(m+1)^2
\Big\|
\mean\Big[
(1-\Theta)^{m+1}
\,
\hat g_{m+1}^{\big(\mean X_1 + \Theta n^{-1} \sum_{i \leq n} \bar X_i \big)}(X)
\Big| X \Big]
\Big\|_{L_2}^2
}{\Var\Big[ \hat g_m^{(\mean X_1)}(X) \Big]}$}}\,,

\displaystyle\alpha_{m,\nu}(k)\coloneqq\bigg\|\msum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}+\ldots+p_{k}=m\\
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\Big\langle\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\partial_m g(\mean X_1)}{m!}$}}\,,\,\overline{(X_{1}-\mathbb{E}X_{1})^{\otimes p_{1}}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\overline{(X_{k}-\mathbb{E}X_{1})^{\otimes p_{k}}}\,\Big\rangle\bigg\|_{L_{\nu}},

\displaystyle\text{ and }\beta_{m,\nu}\;\coloneqq\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{
\sum_{k=1}^m
\binom{n}{k}
(\alpha_{m,\nu}(k))^2
}{
\sum_{k=1}^m
\binom{n}{k}
(\alpha_{m,2}(k))^2
}$}}\;.

\epsilon_{m}
is a variance ratio that is small when the
m
-th order term dominates other terms of Taylor expansion in variance (
Theorem

2
; see
Remark

11
for a further upper bound).
\beta_{m,\nu}
is a V-statistic analogue of the classical Berry-Esséen ratio from
Lemma

8
(see
Remark

9
for a further upper bound). The next result gives a bound for approximating
\hat{g}(X)
by
\hat{g}_{m}^{(\mathbb{E}X_{1})}(Z)
.

Proposition 9
.

Assume that
g
is
(m+1)
-times continuously differentiable and that
\hat{g}^{(\mathbb{E}X_{1})}_{m}
is
\delta
-regular (
Definition

5
) with respect to
(X_{i}-\mathbb{E}X_{1})_{i\leq n}
for some
\delta\in(0,1)
. Then, there is some absolute constant
C>0
such that for all
n,m,d\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq 2m^{2}
and any
\nu\in(2,3]
, we have

\displaystyle\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{P}\big(\hat{g}(X)-\msum_{l=0}^{m}\mu_{l}\leq t\big)-\mathbb{P}\big(\,\hat{g}_{m}^{(\mathbb{E}X_{1})}(Z)-\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{g}_{m}^{(\mathbb{E}X_{1})}(Z)\big]\leq t\,\big)\big|\;\leq\;\Delta_{\hat{g},m}\;,

\displaystyle\qquad\text{where }\quad\Delta_{\hat{g},m}\;\coloneqq\;Cm\Big(\epsilon_{m}^{\frac{1}{2m+1}}+n^{-\frac{1-\delta}{2m+1}}+n^{-\frac{\nu-2}{2\nu m+2}}\,\beta_{m,\nu}^{\frac{\nu}{2\nu m+2}}\Big)\;.

Write
\Phi
as the c.d.f. of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and suppose
\text{\rm Var}\big[\hat{g}_{m}^{(\mathbb{E}X_{1})}(X)\big],\text{\rm Var}\big[\hat{g}_{m}^{(\mathbb{E}X_{1})}(Z)\big]>0
. Then there are some absolute constants
C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\mathbb{P}\Big(\text{\rm Var}\big[\hat{g}_{m}^{(\mathbb{E}X_{1})}(

\displaystyle X)\big]^{-1/2}\big(\hat{g}(X)-\msum_{l=0}^{m}\mu_{l}\big)\leq t\Big)-\Phi(t)\Big|

\displaystyle\;\leq

\displaystyle\;\Delta_{\hat{g},m}+C^{\prime}\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{(C'')^m \, n^{-(1-\delta)/2}}{ 2 - (C'')^m \, n^{-(1-\delta)/2}}$}}+\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{4m-4}{3m}$}}\,\big|\text{\rm Kurt}\big[\hat{g}_{m}^{(\mathbb{E}X_{1})}(Z)\big]\big|\Big)^{1/2}.

Remark 10
.

Proposition

9
considers only the case when one of the derivatives dominates, but can be readily extended to the case when several terms dominate at the same time: This is done by applying variance domination (
Theorem

2
) to the appropriate dominating quantity.

Remark 11

(Upper bound on
\epsilon_{m}
)
.

Using a triangle inequality and a Jensen’s inequality, we can obtain a further upper bound on the first term of
\epsilon_{m}
as

\displaystyle(m-1)\,\sum_{l=1}^{m-1}\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\Var\Big[ \hat g_l^{(\mean X_1)}(X) \Big]}{\Var\Big[ \hat g_m^{(\mean X_1)}(X) \Big]}$}}\;.

Each summand involves variances of V-statistics, whose upper and lower bounds are given in
Lemma

8
. As discussed in
Remarks

8
and
9
, these variance ratios can either be controlled by a further spectral bound or an explicit computation. An analogous bound applies to the second term, provided that the
(m+1)
-th derivative satisfies some stability condition: One example of such a condition is the existence of some
\nu>2
such that

\displaystyle\Big\|\,\hat{g}_{m+1}^{(\mathbb{E}X_{1}+\Theta S_{n})}(X)-\hat{g}_{m+1}^{(\mathbb{E}X_{1})}(X)\,\Big\|_{L_{\nu}}\;=\;o(1)\;.

When
g
is infinitely differentiable,
Proposition

9
implies that the asymptotic distribution of
\hat{g}(X)
is determined by its
m
-th order Taylor expansion for the smallest
m
such that
\epsilon_{m}\rightarrow 0
, i.e. the
m
-th order term that dominates in variance. As such,
Proposition

9
strictly generalises the
m
-th order delta method by replacing the requirement that all lower derivatives are zero with the condition that
\epsilon_{m}=o(1)
, whereas the finite-sample bound introduces a second condition
\beta_{m,\nu}=o(n^{(\nu-2)/2\nu})
analogous to a Berry-Esséen moment ratio. In the high-dimensional regime,
Proposition

9
generalises the observations in
g_{\rm toy}
to show two aspects of delta method that depart from classical behaviours:

•

Non-Gaussianity despite a non-zero first derivative.
The approximation by a degree-
m
polynomial of Gaussians can dominate the first-order Gaussian term, when the first derivative is negligible compared to some
m
-th derivative.
If the excess kurtosis of this degree-
m
Gaussian polynomial does not vanish,
Proposition

4
implies that
\hat{g}(X)
is not asymptotically normal under a further uniform integrability condition. In particular, this may hold despite a non-zero first derivative term.

•

Non-consistency (under the plug-in principle).
By applying a further Markov’s inequality on
\mathbb{P}\big(\,\hat{g}_{m}^{(\mathbb{E}X_{1})}(Z)-\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{g}_{m}^{(\mathbb{E}X_{1})}(Z)\big]\leq t\,\big)
, we see that if
\Delta_{\hat{g},m}=o(1)
and
\text{\rm Var}\big[\hat{g}_{m}^{(\mathbb{E}X_{1})}(Z)\big]=o(1)
, then

\displaystyle\hat{g}(X)-\msum_{l=0}^{m}\mu_{l}\;\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}\;0\;.

Recall that under the plug-in principle,
\hat{g}(X)
is intended as an estimator for
g(\mathbb{E}X_{1})=\mu_{0}
. Since, by linearity,

\displaystyle\mu_{1}\;=\;\mathbb{E}\big[\langle\partial g(\mathbb{E}X_{1}),X_{1}-\mathbb{E}X_{1}\rangle\big]\;=\;0\;,

\hat{g}(X)
is indeed consistent when
Proposition

9
holds with
m=1
(e.g. in the classical case with non-zero first derivative). When
m>1
, however,
\hat{g}(X)
may be non-consistent, and the bias is exactly given by the limit of
\sum_{l=2}^{m}\mu_{l}
.

Remark 12

(Gaussianity under a zero first derivative)
.

Proposition

4
also implies that where
m
and
d
are allowed to grow, a degree-
m
polynomial of
d
-dimensional Gaussians may itself be asymptotically Gaussian: This can be seen from
\hat{g}_{\rm toy}(X)
with
X_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,I_{d})
, whose second-order term satisfies

\displaystyle\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n^2}$}}\msum_{i,j=1}^{n}\bar{X}_{i}^{\top}\bar{X}_{j}\;\overset{d}{=}\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{d}{n}$}}\times\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{d}$}}\msum_{j=1}^{d}\eta_{j}^{2}\qquad\text{ with }\eta_{j}\overset{i.i.d.}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,1)\;.

This is an average of i.i.d. variables, and becomes asymptotically normal when
d
and
n
both grow. A consequence is that in the high-dimensional regime, Gaussianity may also occur even when the first derivative is zero.

Remark 13
.

Theorem

1
and
Theorem

2
in fact apply to a more general version of delta method on
g_{n}(T_{n})
, where we consider a sequence of random variables
\{T_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
, with each
T_{n}
determined by
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
, and a sequence of well-behaved functions
\{g_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
. The only requirement is a suitable Taylor approximation of the estimator, which yields the polynomial structure. Degree-
m
U-statistics, discussed next, give such an example.

Degree-
m
U-statistics

Let
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
be i.i.d. random variables taking values in some general (not necessarily Euclidean), possibly
n
-dependent measurable space
\mathcal{E}\equiv\mathcal{E}(n)
. Given a symmetric function
u:\mathcal{E}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, consider the degree-
m
U-statistic given by

\displaystyle u_{m}(Y)\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n(n-1) \ldots(n-m+1)}$}}\msum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}\in[n]\text{ \rm distinct }}u(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{m}})\;.

(10)

Classically, under a technique called the Hájek projection, the asymptotic distribution of
u_{m}(Y)
can be approximated by that of a degree-
M
polynomial of Gaussians, where

\displaystyle M\;\coloneqq\;\min\{j\in[m]\,:\,\sigma_{j}>0\}\;,\qquad\text{and }\quad\sigma_{j}^{2}\coloneqq\text{\rm Var}\,\mathbb{E}[u(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})\,|\,Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{j}]\;.

See
[
24
]
,
[
53
]
and Chapter 5.4 of
[
55
]
for references on the classical theory for U-statistics. In this section, we show how the high-dimensional asymptotics of
u_{m}(Y)
, where
\mathcal{E}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
m
and
d
allowed to be large relative to
n
, can again be obtained with our general results.

We will invoke a functional decomposition assumption similar to the one used in
[
35
]
to obtain finite-sample bounds. For each
K\in\mathbb{N}
, we consider approximating
u(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m})
by a polynomial of
m
vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{K}
,

\displaystyle\msum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}=1}^{K}\lambda^{(K)}_{k_{1}\ldots k_{m}}\phi^{(K)}_{k_{1}}(y_{1})\times\ldots\times\phi^{(K)}_{k_{m}}(y_{m})\;,

where for each
K
, the coordinates of the
m
vectors are defined via a triangular array of
\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
functions
\{\phi^{(K)}_{k}\}_{k\leq K,K\in\mathbb{N}}
evaluated at
y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}
, and the polynomial weights are given by a triangular array of real values
\{\lambda^{(K)}_{k_{1}\ldots k_{m}}\}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}\leq K,K\in\mathbb{N}}
. We also denote the
L_{\nu}
approximation error as

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{K;\nu}\;\coloneqq\;\big\|\msum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}=1}^{K}\lambda^{(K)}_{k_{1}\ldots k_{m}}\phi^{(K)}_{k_{1}}(Y_{1})\times\ldots\times\phi^{(K)}_{k_{m}}(Y_{m})-u(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})\big\|_{L_{\nu}}\;.

Assumption 1
.

There exists some
\nu\in(2,3]
such that, for every fixed
n
,
m
,
d
and
\mathcal{E}
, as
K\rightarrow\infty
, the error
\varepsilon_{K;\nu}\rightarrow 0
for some choice of
\{\phi^{(K)}_{k}\}_{k\leq K,K\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{\lambda^{(K)}_{k_{1}\ldots k_{m}}\}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}\leq K,K\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Remark 14
.

(i)
1
is very mild: In the appendix, we show how it holds with
\nu=2
under mild assumptions on the
L_{2}
space equipped with the
m
-fold product measure of
Y_{1}
(
Lemma

34
), and how it holds for
\mathcal{E}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
u
well-approximated by a Taylor expansion (
Lemma

35
). (ii) Unlike asymptotic U-statistics results that use the
L_{2}
decomposition from Hilbert-Schmidt operator theory (see e.g. Chapter 5.4 of
[
55
]
),
1
does not require orthogonality or boundedness conditions on
\phi^{(K)}_{k}
or
\lambda^{(K)}_{k_{1}\ldots k_{m}}
and also allow them to vary with
K
or
n
. In particular, they are generally not unique and can be chosen at convenience for verification of the assumption, e.g. by a suitable Taylor expansion.

Penultimate approximation.
To identify the dominating component, we first recall that by Hoeffding’s decomposition theorem (see e.g. Theorem 1.2.1 of
[
20
]
),

\displaystyle u_{m}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\;=\;\mathbb{E}[u(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})]\,+\,\msum_{j=1}^{m}\Bigl(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{m}{j}$}}\Bigr)\,U^{\rm H}_{j}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\;,

(11)

where each Hoeffding’s decomposition
U^{\rm H}_{j}(Y)
is a degenerate degree-
j
U-statistic defined by

\displaystyle U^{\rm H}_{j}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n(n-1) \ldots(n-j+1)}$}}\msum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{j}\in[n]\text{ \rm distinct }}u^{\rm H}_{j}(y_{i_{1}},\ldots,y_{i_{j}})\;,

\displaystyle u^{\rm H}_{j}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{j})\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\msum_{r=0}^{j}(-1)^{j-r}\msum_{1\leq l_{1}<\ldots<l_{r}\leq j}\mathbb{E}\big[u(y_{l_{1}},\ldots,y_{l_{r}},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m-r})\big]\;.

Under
1
, each Hoeffding’s decomposition can be approximated by a polynomial of
\mathbb{R}^{K}
vectors, to which our universality results can be applied. Let
\Xi^{(K)}\coloneqq\{\xi^{(K)}_{1},\ldots,\xi^{(K)}_{n}\}
be a collection of i.i.d. zero-mean Gaussian random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{mK}
with the same variance as
(\phi^{(K)}_{1}(Y_{1}),\ldots,\phi^{(K)}_{K}(Y_{1}))^{\top}
. For
j\in[m]
and
K\in\mathbb{N}
, the penultimate approximation for
U^{\rm H}_{j}(Y)
is therefore given as a polynomial of
\mathbb{R}^{K}
Gaussians,

\displaystyle U^{(K)}_{j}(\Xi^{(K)})\;\coloneqq\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{n(n-1) \ldots(n-j+1)}$}}\msum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{j}\in[n]\text{ \rm distinct }}\tilde{u}_{j}^{(K)}(\xi^{(K)}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\xi^{(K)}_{i_{j}})\;,

(12)

\displaystyle u^{(K)}_{j}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{j})\;\coloneqq\;\msum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}=1}^{K}\,\lambda^{(K)}_{k_{1}\ldots k_{m}}v_{1k_{1}}\cdots v_{jk_{j}}\,\mathbb{E}\big[\phi^{(K)}_{k_{j+1}}(Y_{1})\big]\cdots\mathbb{E}\big[\phi^{(K)}_{k_{m}}(Y_{1})\big]\;.

Moments.
By variance domination, the asymptotic distribution of
u_{m}(Y)
is determined by the relative size of the rescaled variances, given for
M\in[m]
as

\displaystyle\rho_{m,n;M}\;

\displaystyle\coloneqq\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\sum_{r \in[m] \setminus\{M\}} \sigma_{m,n;r}^2 }{\sigma_{m,n;M}^2}$}}\;,

\displaystyle\text{where }\;\sigma_{m,n;j}^{2}\;\coloneqq\;\Bigl(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{m}{j}$}}\Bigr)^{2}\Bigl(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{j}$}}\Bigr)^{-1}\text{\rm Var}\,\mathbb{E}[u(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})\,|\,Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{j}]\qquad\text{ for }j\in[m]\;.

\sigma^{2}_{m,n;j}
describes the contribution of
U^{\rm H}_{j}
to the overall variance of
u_{m}(Y)
(
Lemma

29
). The bound also depends on a Berry-Esséen moment ratio, given for
\nu\in(2,3]
and
M\in[m]
as

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}_{M,\nu}\;\coloneqq\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{
\big\|
u_M^{\rm H} \big( Y_1,\ldots, Y_M \big)
\big\|_{L_\nu}
}{
\big\|
u_M^{\rm H} \big( Y_1,\ldots, Y_M \big)
\big\|_{L_2}
}$}}\;.

Proposition 10
.

Suppose
1
holds for some
\nu\in(2,3]
. Then there is some absolute constant
C>0
such that for every
n,m,d\in\mathbb{N}
and
M\in[m]
, we have

\displaystyle\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\mathbb{P}\big(u_{m}(Y)-\mathbb{E}[u(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})]\leq t\big)-\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\Bigl(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{m}{M}$}}\Bigr)\,U_{M}^{(K)}(\Xi^{(K)})\leq t\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\;\leq\;Cm\Big(n^{-\frac{\nu-2}{2(\nu M+1)}}\tilde{\beta}_{M,\nu}^{\frac{\nu}{\nu M+1}}+\rho_{m,n;M}^{\frac{1}{2M+1}}\Big)\;.

Moreover, writing
\Phi
as the c.d.f. of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, we have that

\displaystyle\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\mathbb{P}\big(

\displaystyle u_{m}(Y)-\mathbb{E}[u(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})]\leq t\big)-\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}\Phi\Big(\Bigl(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{m}{M}$}}\Bigr)^{-1}\text{\rm Var}\big[U_{M}^{(K)}(\Xi^{(K)})\big]^{-1/2}\,t\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\;\leq

\displaystyle\;Cm\Big(n^{-\frac{\nu-2}{2(\nu M+1)}}\beta_{M,\nu}^{\frac{\nu}{\nu M+1}}+\rho_{m,n;M}^{\frac{1}{2M+1}}\Big)+\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{4m-4}{3m}$}}\,\big|\text{\rm Kurt}\big[U_{M}^{(K)}(\Xi^{(K)})\big]\big|\Big)^{1/2}\;.

We emphasise that
Proposition

10
allows the degree of the U-statistic
m
to be much larger than
\log n
: In the above bound, when
\tilde{\beta}_{M,\nu}=O(1)
and
\rho_{m,n;M}=o(1)
, we only require the degree of the polynomial of Gaussians to satisfy
M\log M=o(\log n)
, with no explicit requirement on
m
. This allows
Proposition

10
to recover many known results on high-dimensional U-statistics. Similar to
Proposition

9
, there are two cases under which a normal approximation is allowed, but with different variances:

•

When
M=1
, the distributional limit
\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}U_{1}^{(K)}(\Xi^{(K)})
, when exists, is Gaussian. In this case,
Proposition

10
provides a finite-sample bound for the Gaussian approximation of
u_{m}(Y)
, a potentially infinite-order U-statistic (IOUS). Notably if
\tilde{\beta}_{M,\nu}
is bounded, the only dependence on
m
in the bound is through
\rho_{m,n;M}
; when
d
is fixed and
\text{\rm Var}\,\mathbb{E}[u(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})\,|\,Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{j}]\in(0,\infty)
is fixed for all
j\in[m]
, we can compute

\displaystyle\rho_{m,n;1}\;\leq\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{n}{m^2}$}}\msum_{r=2}^{m}\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{m^2 e^2}{n}$}}\Big)^{r}\;=\;O\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{m^2}{n}$}}\Big)\hskip 50.00008pt\text{ if }\;m^{2}<\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{n}{2e^2}$}}\;.

Therefore when
d
is fixed and
M=1
, Gaussianity holds if
m=o(n^{-1/2})
. This agrees with known optimal growth condition on
m
for Gaussianity of 1d IOUS
[
60
]
. On the other hand, when
d
and the variances are allowed to change,
\rho_{m,n;1}
has more complex dependence on
n
; this can lead to a more stringent or relaxed condition on the size of
m
.

•

When
M>1
,
U_{M}^{(K)}(\Xi^{(K)})
is a degree-
M
polynomial of Gaussians.
By the fourth moment phenomenon (
Proposition

4
), the asymptotic limit can still be Gaussian in high dimensions, for which
Proposition

9
provides a finite-sample bound. This is a behaviour specific to the high-dimensional regime, where the Gaussian polynomial approximation is allowed to vary in
n
.

The above two cases recover many existing works on the normal approximation of a high-dimensional degree-two U-statistic: Some of those results come without a fourth-moment condition and require assumptions similar to those for (i)
[
15
,
32
,
63
,
65
]
, whereas others consider a fourth moment condition and fall under (ii)
[
25
,
5
]
. A practical consequence of (ii) is that a degenerate U-statistic – often found in hypothesis testing and which requires numerical approximation due to its classical non-Gaussianity
[
41
]
– may be Gaussian in high dimensions. In view of the necessity statement in
Proposition

4
, one may argue that the two cases cover most, if not all of the situations of Gaussianity.

Meanwhile, as with
Proposition

9
,
Proposition

10
highlights when
u_{m}(Y)
may be asymptotically non-Gaussian. Here, non-Gaussianity happens not according to the degeneracy of the U-statistic, but depending on the relative sizes of the rescaled variances
\sigma_{m,n;j}^{2}
. This has been highlighted in the literature for specific statistics
[
5
,
35
]
. We also refer readers to
[
35
]
for empirical evidence of the transition of a
U
-statistic between different limits in high dimensions: There, variance domination reduces to problem-specific hyperparameter choices.

Remark 15
.

The same argument in this section can be extended to a U-statistic of non-identically distributed data, although a more elaborate decomposition than (
11
) is required to accommodate for the fact that
\mathbb{E}[u(y_{1},\ldots,y_{r},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m-r})]
may not be equal to
\mathbb{E}[u(y_{1},\ldots,y_{r},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{m-r+1})]
.
Section

3.3
illustrates such an example.

Higher-order fluctuations of subgraph counts

We apply our results to characterise the possible asymptotic distributions of subgraph counts. We shall see that variance domination recovers and extends the geometric conditions considered by Hladkỳ et al.
[
33
]
and Bhattacharya et al.
[
4
]
for obtaining different limits, and our results also apply to the edge-level fluctuations considered by Kaur and Röllin
[
37
]
.

Graph model.
Given a symmetric, measurable function
w:[0,1]^{2}\rightarrow[0,1]
, we generate a graph
G_{n}
with vertex set
[n]
by drawing independent (not necessarily identically distributed) random variables
(U_{i})_{i\leq n}
and
(V_{ij})_{1\leq i<j\leq n}
, and joining an edge
i\sim j
according to

\displaystyle Y_{ij}\;\coloneqq\;\mathbb{I}_{\{V_{ij}\leq w(U_{i},U_{j})\}}\;.

When
U_{i},V_{ij}\overset{\rm i.i.d.}{\sim}\textrm{Uniform}[0,1]
, this generates an exchangeable graph from
w

[
22
]
.

Subgraph count statistics.
Denote
K(S)
as the complete graph on the vertex set
S\subseteq[n]
,
K_{n}\coloneqq K(\{1,\ldots,n\})
and
E(H^{\prime})
as the edge set of a graph
H^{\prime}
. Given a non-empty simple graph
H
with
m=m(n)
vertices and
k=k(n)
edges, we are interested in the number of subgraphs in
G_{n}
that are isomorphic to
H
, described by

\displaystyle\kappa(Y)\;\coloneqq\;\msum_{1\leq i_{1}<\ldots<i_{m}\leq n}\;\msum_{H^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{H}(\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}\})}\;\mprod_{(i_{s},i_{t})\in E(H^{\prime})}Y_{i_{s}i_{t}}\,,

where
\mathcal{G}_{H}(S)
denotes the collection of all subgraphs of
K(S)
that are isomorphic to
H
.

Consider the conditionally centred indicator variable
\bar{Y}_{ij}\coloneqq Y_{ij}-w(U_{i},U_{j})
. To derive the asymptotic limit of
\kappa(Y)
, we first split
\kappa(Y)
into a sum over vertices and a sum over edges:

\displaystyle\kappa(Y)\;=

\displaystyle\;\msum_{1\leq i_{1}<\ldots<i_{m}\leq n}\;\msum_{H^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{H}(\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}\})}\;\mprod_{(i_{s},i_{t})\in E(H^{\prime})}w(U_{i_{s}},U_{i_{t}})

\displaystyle\;+\msum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{(i_{1},j_{1}),\ldots,(i_{k},j_{k})\}\subseteq E(K_{n})\\
\text{is a set of distinct edges}\end{subarray}}\;\delta_{H}\big(\{(i_{s},j_{s})\}_{s\in[k]}\big)\,\mprod_{s=1}^{k}\bar{Y}_{i_{s}j_{s}}\;\eqqcolon\;\kappa_{1}(U)+\kappa_{2}(\bar{Y})\;,

where
\delta_{H}(I)
is the indicator function on whether the graph formed by the edge set
I
is isomorphic to
H
.
\kappa_{1}(U)

represents vertex-level fluctuations, and
\kappa_{2}(\bar{Y})
represents edge-level fluctuations. We first study the two fluctuations separately, and show how they affect the distribution on
\kappa(Y)
by variance domination.

Vertex-level fluctuations.
Let
U\coloneqq(U_{i})_{i\leq n}
be i.i.d. for notational simplicity.
\kappa_{1}(U)
is now the degree-
m
U-statistic considered in
Proposition

10
, with the kernel

\displaystyle u_{1}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})\;\coloneqq\;\Bigl(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{m}$}}\Bigr)\;\msum_{H^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{H}(\{1,\ldots,m\})}\;\mprod_{(i_{s},i_{t})\in E(H^{\prime})}w(x_{i_{s}},x_{i_{t}})\;.

Applying
Proposition

10
directly gives the following corollary, which approximates
\kappa_{1}(U)
by its
M
-th Hoeffding decomposition. The notation
U^{(K)}_{M}(\Xi^{(K)})
,
\tilde{\beta}_{M,\nu}
,
\sigma^{2}_{m,n;M}
and
\rho_{m,n;M}
are defined in terms of the U-statistic
\kappa_{1}(U)
.

Corollary 11
.

Suppose
1
holds for the kernel function
u_{1}
with some
\nu\in(2,3]
. Then there is some absolute constant
C>0
s.t. for every
n,m,d\in\mathbb{N}
and
M\in[m]
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\;\Big|\mathbb{P}\big(\kappa_{1}(U)-\mathbb{E}[\kappa_{1}(U)]\leq t\big)-\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\Big(

\displaystyle\,\Bigl(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{m}{M}$}}\Bigr)U_{M}^{(K)}(\Xi^{(K)})\leq t\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\;\leq\;Cm\Big(n^{-\frac{\nu-2}{2(\nu M+1)}}\tilde{\beta}_{M,\nu}^{\frac{\nu}{\nu M+1}}+\rho_{m,n;M}^{\frac{1}{2M+1}}\Big)\;.

The variance ratio
\rho_{m,n;M}=\frac{\sum_{r\in[m]\setminus M}\sigma_{m,n;r}^{2}}{\sigma_{m,n;M}^{2}}
can be computed in terms of

\displaystyle\sigma_{m,n;r}^{2}\;=

\displaystyle\;\Bigl(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{m}{r}$}}\Bigr)\Bigl(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{m}$}}\Bigr)\Bigl(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n-r}{m-r}$}}\Bigr)\,\text{\rm Var}\,\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\sum_{H^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{H}(\{1,\ldots,m\})}\;\prod_{(i_{s},i_{t})\in E(H^{\prime})}w(U_{i_{s}},U_{i_{t}})\,\Bigg|\,U_{1},\ldots,U_{r}\Bigg]\;.

Remark 16
.

A common tool for proving limit theorems for subgraph counts
[
37
,
4
]
is the orthogonal decomposition of a generalised U-statistic proposed by Janson and Nowicki
[
36
]
. Here, this assumption takes the form of
1
: the difference is that we focus only on vertices, and perform a decomposition in an
L_{\nu}
space with
\nu>2
(see
Remark

14
).

As before, the size of
\sigma_{m,n;M}^{2}
– the variance of the
M
-th order Hoeffding decomposition – determines whether
\kappa_{1}(U)
can be approximated by a degree-
M
polynomial of Gaussians, This is known in the literature as the
n^{m-\frac{M}{2}}
-th order fluctuation
[
37
]
, as evident from the
\binom{n}{m}\binom{n-M}{m-M}=O(n^{2m-M})
factor in
\sigma^{2}_{m,n;M}
. A guiding example is when
H=K_{2}
, i.e.
\kappa(Y)
is the edge count: In this case, the variances of the Gaussian component and the quadratic-form-of-Gaussian component are respectively

\displaystyle\sigma_{m,n;1}^{2}\;=

\displaystyle\;n(n-1)^{2}\,\text{\rm Var}\,\mathbb{E}[w(U_{1},U_{2})|U_{1}]

and

\displaystyle\sigma_{m,n;2}^{2}\;=

\displaystyle\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{n(n-1)}{2}$}}\text{\rm Var}[w(U_{1},U_{2})]\;,

which are the variances of the
n^{3/2}
-th order fluctuation and the
n
-th order fluctuation.

The next result gives conditions under which variance domination by the Gaussian component (
M=1
, or
n^{m-\frac{1}{2}}
-th order fluctuation) fails.

Lemma 12
.

Let
w
,
H
and
m\geq 2
be fixed. Then
\sigma^{2}_{m,n;r}=O(n^{2m-r})
for all
r\in[m]
. Moreover, the following statements are equivalent:

(i)

\rho^{2}_{m,n;1}=\Omega(1)
;

(ii)

\sigma^{2}_{m,n;1}=0
;

(iii)

\sum_{H^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{H}(\{1,\ldots,m\})}\;\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{(i_{s},i_{t})\in E(H^{\prime})}w(U_{i_{s}},U_{i_{t}})\,\Big|\,U_{1}\Big]

is constant almost surely;

(iv)

For almost every
x\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{m}$}}\msum_{i=1}^{m}\;\mathbb{E}\Big[\mprod_{(i_{s},i_{t})\in E(H)}w(U_{i_{s}},U_{i_{t}})\,\Big|\,U_{i}=x\Big]\;=\;\mathbb{E}\Big[\mprod_{(i_{s},i_{t})\in E(H)}w(U_{i_{s}},U_{i_{t}})\Big]\;.

Lemma

12
(i) is the condition derived from variance domination, whereas
Lemma

12
(iv) is the definition of
H
-regularity of
w
, introduced by
[
33
]
for a complete subgraph
H
and extended to a general subgraph
H
by
[
4
]
. Indeed, Theorem 1.2 of
[
33
]
and Theorem 2.9 of
[
4
]
establish that
\kappa_{1}(U)
exhibit fluctuations of an order higher than
n^{m-\frac{1}{2}}
if and only if
H
-regularity holds – a geometric condition that is recovered by variance domination. Meanwhile, variance domination provides more: We can now characterise all fluctuations in
\kappa_{1}(U)
on the orders
n^{m-\frac{1}{2}},n^{m-1},\ldots,n^{\frac{m}{2}}
, all with finite-sample bounds.
Corollary

11
may also be applied to a setup where
w
and
H
are allowed to vary in
n
.

Remark 17
.

In the case of
H=K_{2}
,
Lemma

12
(iii) says that
\mathbb{E}[w(U_{1},U_{2})\,|\,U_{1}]
is constant almost surely. In other words, the edge count statistic
\kappa(Y)
exhibits
n
-th order fluctuations if and only if the random graph is regular on average.

Edge-level fluctuations.
In practice, one may want to analyse edge properties of the random graph, which requires us to study
\kappa_{2}(\bar{Y})
. Kaur and Röllin
[
37
]
provide error bounds on the Gaussian approximation of
\kappa_{2}(\bar{Y})
by using the orthogonal decomposition of a generalised U-statistic of
U
and
V
proposed by
[
36
]
. We consider a variant of their setup. For convenience, we re-index
(\bar{Y}_{ij})_{i,j\in E(K_{n})}
as
\bar{Y}_{1},\ldots,\bar{Y}_{n_{*}}
, where
n_{*}=|E(K_{n})|=\binom{n}{2}
; recall that
k
is the number of edges of
H
. Conditioning on
U=(U_{i})_{i\leq n}
, let
Z=(Z_{i})_{i\leq n_{*}}
be conditionally normal variables defined by

\displaystyle Z_{i}\,|\,U\;\;\overset{i.i.d.}{\sim}\;\;\mathcal{N}\big(\mathbb{E}[\bar{Y}_{i}\,|\,U]\,,\,\text{\rm Var}[\bar{Y}_{i}\,|\,U]\big)\;.

The penultimate approximation for
\kappa_{2}(\bar{Y})
is the incomplete U-statistic

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}(Z)\;=\;\msum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}\}\subseteq E(K_{n})\\
\text{is a set of distinct edges}\end{subarray}}\;\delta_{H}\big(\{e_{s}\}_{s\in[k]}\big)\,\mprod_{s=1}^{k}Z_{e_{s}}\;.

Proposition 13
.

If
\text{\rm Var}[\kappa_{2}(Z)\,|\,U]>0
almost surely, there exist some absolute constant
C>0
such that for every
n\geq 2
and
m,k\in\mathbb{N}
, almost surely,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\sup\nolimits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\mathbb{P}\big(\kappa_{2}(\bar{Y})\leq t\,\big|\,U\big)-\mathbb{P}\big(\kappa_{2}(Z)\leq t\,\big|\,U\big)\Big|\;\leq\;\Delta_{\bar{Y}}(U)\;,

where
\Delta_{\bar{Y}}(U)\coloneqq C\,m\,n^{-\frac{\nu-2}{\nu k+1}}\,\rho_{\bar{Y}}(U)^{\frac{1}{\nu k+1}}

and

\displaystyle\rho_{\bar{Y}}(U)\coloneqq\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\mmax_{i_1, \ldots, i_k \in[n_*] \text{ distinct}} \, \mprod_{l=1}^{k} \mean\big[ \big| \bar Y_{i_l} \big|^\nu\, \big|\, U \big] }{\mmin_{i_1, \ldots, i_{k'} \in[n_*] \text{ distinct}} \, \mprod_{l=1}^{k'} \mean\big[ \big| \bar Y_{i_l} \big|^2 \, \big|\, U \big]^{\nu/2} }$}}\;.

Moreover, writing
\Phi
as the c.d.f. of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, we have that almost surely

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\mathbb{P}\big(\kappa_{2}(Y)-\mathbb{E}[\kappa_{2}(Y)\,|\,U]\leq t\,\big|\,U\big)-\Phi\big(

\displaystyle\text{\rm Var}[\kappa_{2}(Z)\,|\,U]^{-1/2}\,t\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\;\leq

\displaystyle\;\Delta_{\bar{Y}}(U)+\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{4m-4}{3m}$}}\,\big|\text{\rm Kurt}[\kappa_{2}(Z)\,|\,U]\big|\Big)^{1/2}\;,

where we defined

\text{\rm Kurt}[\kappa_{2}(Z)|U]\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[(\kappa_{2}(Z)-\mathbb{E}[\kappa_{2}(Z)|U])^{4}|U]\,/\,\text{\rm Var}[\kappa_{2}(Z)|U]^{2}-3
.

Remark 18
.

(i) Observe that
\kappa(Y)-\mathbb{E}[\kappa(Y)|U]=\kappa_{2}(\bar{Y})
almost surely, since
\kappa_{1}(U)
is fixed under the conditioning on
U
. (ii)
\kappa_{2}(\bar{Y})
is an incomplete U-statistic, where the asymmetry arises from
\delta_{H}
, and
(\bar{Y}_{i})_{i\leq n_{*}}
conditioning on
U
are non-identically distributed. This is an example of how our main results can be applied to asymmetric estimators and non-identically distributed data.

Remark 19
.

The max-min ratio
\rho_{\bar{Y}}
can be avoided by directly using the error bound from
Theorem

1
: In exchange, the bound is given as a sum of non-identical moment terms and the convergence rate cannot be read off directly. This tradeoff is common for asymmetric estimators and non-identically distributed data, although simplifications may be possible for specific statistics.

Overall fluctuations.
We now consider the asymptotic distribution of
\kappa(Y)=\kappa_{1}(U)+\kappa_{2}(\bar{Y})
. Specifically, we investigate whether the vertex-level fluctuations
\kappa_{1}(U)
or the edge-level fluctuations
\kappa_{2}(\bar{Y})
dominates in variance in
\kappa(Y)
. The variances of the different Hoeffding’s decompositions of
\kappa_{1}(U)
have been provided in
Lemma

12
, whereas the next result provides the variance of
\kappa_{2}(\bar{Y})
.

Lemma 14
.

There are some absolute constants
c,C>0
such that

\displaystyle\text{\rm Var}[\kappa_{2}(\bar{Y})]\;\leq

\displaystyle\;C^{k}\,|\mathcal{G}_{H}([n])|\;\mathbb{E}\Big[\max\nolimits_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\in[n_{*}]\text{ distinct}}\,\mprod_{l=1}^{k}\text{\rm Var}[\bar{Y}_{i_{l}}\,|\,U]\Big]\;,

\displaystyle\text{\rm Var}[\kappa_{2}(\bar{Y})]\;\geq

\displaystyle\;c^{k}\,|\mathcal{G}_{H}([n])|\;\mathbb{E}\Big[\min\nolimits_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\in[n_{*}]\text{ distinct}}\,\mprod_{l=1}^{k}\text{\rm Var}[\bar{Y}_{i_{l}}\,|\,U]\Big]\;.

Consider again the setup with
w
,
H
,
m
and
k
fixed. Let
\rm{Aut}(H)
be the set of all automorphisms of
H
. Provided that the expectation terms in
Lemma

14
are
\Theta(1)
, we have

\displaystyle\text{\rm Var}[\kappa_{2}(\bar{Y})]\;=\;\Theta(|\mathcal{G}_{H}([n])|)\;=\;\Theta\Big(\Bigl(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{m}$}}\Bigr)\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{m!}{|\rm{Aut}(H)|}$}}\Big)\;=\;\Theta(n^{m})\;.

Meanwhile, when the dominating term of
\kappa_{1}(U)
is its
M
-th Hoeffding’s decomposition,
\sigma^{2}_{m,n;M}
is assumed to be non-zero, and
Lemma

12
implies

\sigma_{m,n;M}^{2}\;=\;\Theta(n^{2m-M})\;.

As a result,
\kappa_{1}(U)
always dominates
\kappa_{2}(\bar{Y})
in variance when
M<m
. This agrees with the observation in
[
37
]
, who provide a detailed illustration for simple subgraphs. Consequently, analysing the distribution of
\kappa(Y)
usually means that edge-level randomness is ignored except for the case with the highest level of degeneracy.

Some difficulties persist in the most general case: (i) Due to asymmetry and non-identically distributed data, the bounds can be loose or not immediately interpretable, as discussed in
Remark

19
.
Under specific random graph models, bounds can simplify considerably
[
33
]
.
(ii) Even if a penultimate approximation is established, the limiting distribution for an asymmetric polynomial of Gaussians is highly dependent on the weights and may not be immediately obvious.
Different choices of such penultimate approximations can yield natural graph-based interpretations
[
37
,
4
]
. In concurrent work, Chatterjee, Dan, and Bhattacharya
[
12
]

obtain limiting distributions for graphon models with higher-order degeneracies and for joint subgraph counts, and additional geometric insights.

Proof of Theorem
1

The proof idea is standard: We first compare the distributions on a class of smooth functions via Lindeberg’s principle (e.g. Chapter 9 of
[
61
]
). The smooth function bound is then combined with the Carbery-Wright inequality
[
11
]
, an anti-concentration bound for a polynomial of Gaussians, to obtain the bound in the Kolmogorov metric. In this section, we also include several lemmas that will simplify subsequent proofs.
